FURTHER JAPANESE CABINET CHANGES
Radio Tbkyo has announced that a third reshufrle
of the Japanese Cabinet took place on February 2l, as follows:
Finance Minister. Juichi Tsushima replaced Sotaro
Ishiwata as minister. Tsushima ranks with
Ishiwata as an outstanding financier and was
894.002/2-
for many years Wice-President of the Bank of
Japan and has more recently been President
of the North China Development Company.
Chief Cabinet Secretary and Minister without
Portfolio. Sotero Ishiwata replaced Hieatada
Hirose who assumed office February l0 and
resigned because "he found himself unable
to agree to some extent with Premier Koiso
2245
regarding the operation of the Regional
Administrative Councils", recently aligned
with Army Districts.
Notwithstanding the announced reason for Hirose's
resignation and the fluid political condition in Japan at
present, it seems worth speculating whether the changes may
7
indicate unity(rather than dissension) and a feeling on the
S
part of the leading political elements in Japan that Japan's
military prospects will be furthered in terms of domestic
morale and of reactions in occupied areas if Koiso could
894.002
remain as Premier in spite of recent severe military
reverses. Tsushima's appointment is in line with Japan
Ud
determination
112
2
au
22
Uut
Dist
45

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
determination and admitted need to exploit in full the
economio recources of its continental base. The addition
of another financial and business expert to the Cabinet should
strengthen the Government's oapacity to meet two of its
major problems: (1) to increase industrial production,
and (2) to further the production and distribution of
foodstuffs.
Radio Tokyo reports that the reaction of the Japanese
press has been especially favorable to the appointment of
Ishiwata because of the need for a man of hie wide experienoe
and tested politicel ability as Chief Cabinet Seoretary, an
office so important in coordinating the war effort.

JAPANESE CARINET CHANGES
In a partial reshuffle of the Japanese Cabinet on
February lo the following changes were made:
Education Ministry. Count Hideo Kodama, Minister
without Portfolio and concurrently acting Minister of
894.002/2
Education became Minister. Kodama had been acting
Minister since the resignation on January 26 due to
1llness of Lt. General Harushige Ninomiya.
Welfare Miniatry. Katsuroku Aikawa, Vice Minister
of Welfare was elevated to the position of Minister,
replacing Hisatada Hirose.
Minister vithout Portfolie and Cabinet Secretary.
Hisatada Hirose was appointed Minister without Portfolio
and concurrently succeeded as Chier Cabinet Secretary
2
Takeo Tanaka.
5
The changes have strengthened the personnel of the
Koiso Government, but indicate no radical deviation in
policy. Civilian representation was broadened by Kodama's
7
appointment, but he has long been identified with military
elements. The effectiveness of the important post of Chier
Cabinet Secretary was increased by the appointment of Hirose,
who was Welfare Minister in the Hiranuma Cabinet in 1939 and
Director of the Legislation Bureau of the Yonai Cabinet of
894.00
1940, whereas Tanaka's earlier experience was largely in
Korea as an associate of Koiso. The changes do not approach
in importance those made by former Premier General Tojo
80
following the fall of Saipan but may nevertheless indicate
2
that Premier Koiso may attempt to retain office notwithstanding
45
the loss of Manila.

THE SUZUKI CABINET
Installed April 7, 1945
The most significant difference between the composi-
tion of the Koiso Cabinet and the incoming Suzuki Cabinet
1s a shift from personnel primarily fitted to carry on
aggressive war abroad to personnel primarily fitted for
home defense. However, the large number of "moderatos"
who have been politicaily in the baokground during Japan
expansion era, means that its eventual use in an attempt to
negotiate peace cannot be ruled out. While the Kwantung
military clivue is represented only by one Minister Without
Portfolio the key military positions are still held by
their top renerals from China and Manchuria, and the
894.002/4-
Minister of Traneportation and Communications, Naoto
Kohiyama, has been closely associated with the Kwantung
cligue as President of the South Manchuris Railway.
Admiral Baron Suzuk1, the new Premier, is 77 years of
are and penerelly remarde as a "moderate". His long as-
ooiation ith court circles suggests that an important
reason for his sclection may have been the oonviction that
he can be depended on to do his utmost to preserve the
Throne under all ciroumstances.
12
Admiral Toyoda, Minister of Munitions--expert teohnical
adviser on ordnance in the Tojo and Koiso Cabinets, Presi-
Ent of the Jepn Steel Works, and a Mitsui relative--shouldU
rive the Cabinet strength in mansging industry to fit the
needs of the military ithout creating undue friction be-
tween the Army and the industrislists.
CS
Foreign Minister Togo has the reputation of being well
S/L
liked by the Soviets. Emphasis on this point in Tokyo
broadcasts is consistent with earlier brosdcasts expressing m
a desire to maintain peaceful relations despite Soviet 
Russia's denuncistion of the Neutrality Pact. Togo waso
Foreign Minister at the time of Pearl Harbor.
更
The choice of Chief Cabinet Secretary Hisatsune Siomizu
financial expert and advocate of state control of industry
and finance, suggests that the Cabinet may attempt to out
nn.2
which
R. m

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
which have impeded complete nationalization of Japen
internal eoonomy.
The new Cabinet includes a large proportion of
ministers who are not well-known political leaders.
This, however, is not necessarily an indication of
weakness. At this crucial stage of the war Japanese
leadership needs to be forceful and hardly has to de-
pend on political adroitness in spurring the Japanese
people to greater efforts.

MONON，INDIANA
April 27, 1945
Mr. Erle R. Dickover, Cnief
Japanese Division
Department of State
Washington 25, D. C.
Dear Mr. Dickover:
For several years 1 have attempted to keep on file
894.00/4-
a reasonably accurate register of cabinet and diplomatic
oificials of the various nations. As you can imagine, it
keeps me pretty busy running down contirmation of the changes
in personnel which take place constantly.
No sooner had 1 completed my list of the members
of General Koiso's Government than the General stepped down!
Up to this juncture, I have been unable to locate a complete
and reliable list of the members of Admiral Suzuki's admini-
stration. Youroffice, 1 assume, keeps up with Nipponese
developments. Would it be possible for you, then, to pro-
745
vide the names of the present Japanese Ministers?
Any information you could provide me on l) the
names of the diplomatic chiefs of mission accredited by the
Japanese Government and 2) the names of the diplomatic
chiets of mission resident in Tokyo would also be greatly
appreciated.
Thanking you in advance for your courtesy and co-
operation, 1 am
594.00
sbe

My dear Mr. Sohoonover:
I have reoeived your letter of April 27, 1946
in which you asked for: (1) a list or the members of
the Suzuk1 Cabinet, (2) the names of Japanese Ambassadore
and Ministers accredited to other nations, and (3) the
names of Ambassadors and Hinisters mooredited to the
Japanese Government.
I am enolosing the lists you requested. You will.
of course, realize that beoause of the war the Department
1s not in a position to obtain entirely accurate informa-
tion on these matters and oonsequently oan asaume no
responsibility for the correotness of the lists. You will
note that the lists include Japanese envoys who may by
now not even nominally hold their positione as they were
acoredited to regimes the territories of whioh have been
liberated.
The Department 1s glad to have thie opportunity of
being of assistance and hopes that the enolosed material
will be useful to you.
Sincerely yours,
ERS
Erle R. Dickover
Chief
Division of Japanese Affairs

Field report from Berne, Switzerland
The source of the following information is reported to be
DC/R.
894.002/7-2445
Sasamoto, Beme correspondent for the Japanese newspaper
"Asahi Shimbun":
In the event of negotiations for peace with
the Allies, it is reported that Okada will
be the important figure, as Hiranuma and
Konoye no longer have any influence in Jap-
anese affairs.
General Wakamatsu, who was recently deputy
War Minister, is now in charge of the Jap-
anese Home Defense. Wakamatsu was formerly
in Budapest and later on was in the Office
for War Information, Tokyo. He is not an
extreme militarist.

ASSISTANT SECRETABY
Department of State
THE HIGASHI-KUNI CABINET
Installed August 17, 1945
The Cabinet of Prince Naruhiko HIGASHI-KUNI represents
an effort to form a Government whioh in the eyes of the
Japanese people will carry the full weight of the Emperor's
prestige and will thus secure obedience from the armed foroes
and prevent internal disorder. The objectives of Japan's
leaders appear clearly to be maintenance of the power and
prestige of the Throne and the continuance of Japan's "national
polity". The members of the present Cabinet have been chosen
for their ability to preserve unity and order in the country
and for their knowledge and experience in Government adminis-
Cental
tration.
Two facts are without precedent in the formation of the
Document Must Be Returned To
Cabinet: (1) the personal selection of a Prime Minister by
the Emperor without consultation with elder statesmen; (2) the
selection of a prince of the blood as Prime Minister. Both
894.002/8-
of these acts bring the Emperor nearer to actual administra-
tion of the country and therefore may be cslculated to secure
the maximum loyal cooperation of the Japanese people. Prince
HIGASHI-KUNI also has an impressive military record which may
further his task in securing obedience to the capitulation
order by Japan's armed forces throughout Asia.
Two former premiers have accepted posts in the new Cabi-
net, Prince Fumimaro KONOYE and Admiral Mitsumasa YONAI. Both
have enjoyed reputations as "moderates" and may have been
1
1745
chosen for their expected ability to get along with the Allied
victors, al though it is to be remembered that YONAI belonged
to the KOISO and SUZUKI cabinets.
C
5
Of the thirteen Cabinet members, eight have previously
held Cabinet posts and two have been Cabinet Advisers. The
Fore1gn Minister, SHIGEMITSU, 1s of course a well-known pro-
fessional diplomat who served in the same capacity under TOJO
and KOISO. The Finance and Home Ministers are professional
894.002
civil servants with long experience; thc Ministers of Justice,
Agriculture, and Elucation-Welfare also have good technical
qualifications and are members of the Diet of long standing.
Business is represented by Naoto KOHIYAMA, Transportation
Minister who was president of the Showa Steel Works andthe
2
South Manchurian Railway, and by Chikuhei NAKAJIMA, foude
of the ill-fated Nakajima Aircraft Company, both of who
represent the class of industrialists closely allied toh
military.
592
CS/H
It is

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
It is interesting to recall that in December, 1944
rumors were rife in Chungking that the Japanese were prepared
to cease hostilities 1n China and sue for peace with the Allies.
Among the small group of Japanese named as instigators of this
"peace plan" were Prince HIGASHI-KUNI, Prince KONOYE, and
Admiral YONAI, all members of the present Government. Present
developments suggest that there may have been some basis in
fact for the rumors of that time.
It is questionable that the political orientation of the
new Japanese Government will be favorable to our ultimate
intentions in Japan. However, it does seem clear that the
present Government is in a good position to secure the immediate,
technical fulfillment of the Fotsdam terms. It is likely that
the HIGASHI-KUNI Government will be able to maintain internal
order and discipline for the time being and that our task of
occupation, at least during its first period, may thereby be
facilitated.
EJohansen

:mls
HKullgren

prodding and as such does not necessar-

ness to comply with Allied demands.

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)

HIGASHI-KUNI CABINET
(NAKAJIMA, cont'd.)
Councillor, IRAPS; Nov. 1944, House of
Representatives; Nakajima was a leader
of the former Seiyukai political party.
In 1938-1939, a serious split developed
between two factions, one known as the
orthodox faction headed by Fusanosuke
Kuhara and supporter of Kwantung Army
and wealthy industrialist and another
known as the renovationist faction headd
by Nakajima. As a result, the Seiyukai
was virtually split into two distint po-
litical parties. There was little fun-
damental difference in the policies of
the factions. The issue was primarily
provoked by personal rivalry between
Kuhara and Nakajima for control of the
Seiyukai. Nakajima is virtually the pol-
itical protegé of Field March Count
Juichi Terauchi, farmer C.I.C. of all
Japanese ground forces in the Southern
Regions. Following the February 26th
military uprising in 1936, the Okada
Cabinet resigned and Koki Hirota was
named to form a new government. The
Army at once compelled Hirota to make
Terauchi his War Minister. On 6 March
1936, before Hirota could select his
other Ministers of State, Terauchi sub-
mitted a memorandum in which he listed
certain civilian leaders the Army desiread
in the Cabinet, among whom was Chikuhei
Nakajima. Although Nakajima was not
included, Hirota accepted most of the
other recommended names.

MINISTER OF NAVY
MINISTER OF NAVY
Mitsumasa YONAI, Admiral
Mitsumasa YONAI, Admiral
Minister of Navy in Koiso, Cabinet.
Born 1880; reputedly one of best ship-
handlers in Japanese Navy; expert of
Chinese and Russian affairs; Navy Minis-
ter under Hayashi, Konoye and Hiranuma,
1937-39; Prem1er, January -July, 1940;
Navy Minister under Koiso and Suzuki.
Admiral Yonai is well known as a moder-
ate conservative leader. When Premier
in 1940, he made a desperate effort to
keep Japan out of the Axis alliance and
to prevent wholesale totalitarian change
at home. As a result of the extremist
opposition, however, his Cabinet was
overthrown in July. Just before the
Cabinet fell, the Metropolitan Police
authorities frustrated a plot in which
Yona1 was scheduled for assassination.
Yonai is thoroughly familiar with the
West. He speaks both English and
Russian with fair fluency and has a
good command of Chinese. It is worthy
of note that Yonai had retired from
active service in 1939, but by a special
Imperial order was returned to active
service so that he could become Navy
Minister in the Koiso Cabinet.

Konoe 1s a member of distinguished
noble family, descended from the FUJI-

House and other high aristocracy. His
father was also President, House of
Peers, and an intimate of Prince Saionji
;because his father's being hounded fer
debt, the present Prince Konoe became
a radical and for a time, a believer in
State socialism. He maintained, how-
ever, a high regard for big business,
which considered him a moderating influ-
ence, and backed him as Prime Minister
in 1937, hoping that he would be a
check upon the Army's aggressive ten-
dencies. Actually, he put through for
the Army, in spite of a reluotant Diet,
the Invasion of China, and immense
rearmament program, and the totalitarian
National Mobilization Bill. The Army
backed him and used him as a screen,
when big business wormed its way into
the IRAA in an attempt to oppose his
totalitarian tendencies. When Hitler's
successes strengthened the Japanese
Army's prestige, business again turned
to Konoe who, for the time being, was
playing a moderate role versus the Army,
and built him up as the saviour of the
Nation, hoping once more that he could
be used against the Army. The Army
contiiued to regard him as their man,

entanglement illustrates Konoe's talent

September 18, 1945
Ur. Acheson
The resignation of Mamoru Shigemitsu as Minister of
Foreign Affairs and the appointment to that office of
Shigeru Yoshida may be interpreted as a move to make the
government more pro-Allied and liberal by eliminating from
Returned
the present government all ministere who have held ofrice
since December 7, 1941.
894.002/9-1
With reference to the war records of Shigemitsu and
Yoshida, 1t is significant that Shigemitsu served as Foreign
Minister in the bellicose military cabinets of Tojo and Koiso.
In contrast, Yoshida held no official position throughout the
war.
Prior to the outbreak of hostilities, Yoshida was reo-
ognized as a distinguished, able and moderate career diplomat.
After graduating from the Tokyo Imperial University, 1906,
he entered the Foreign Service, serving in various posts in
8
As1a, Europe and Amer1ca. In 1916-1917 he was Second Seore-
tary to the Japanese Embassy in Washington. He was appointed
Minister to Sweden in 1928 and was Ambassador to Italy from
5
193 to 1932. He served as Ambassador to England 1936, 1939.
Yoshida's claim to be a liberal 1s based on his associa-
tion as Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs under the moderate
Foreign Minister Baron Shidehara; to his support of the London
Naval Conference of 1930; to the Army's opposition to his
S
designation as Foreign Minister by Premier Hirota following
WW
the February incident of 1936; and to his retirement from
public life during the war.
Yoshida is married to the daughter of the moderate late
Count Makino, formerly Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, who
94,002
escaped assassination at the hands of the military extremists
in the February 1936 revolt.
In considering

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
In considering the nature and extent of Mr. Yoshida's
influence in the Government, it should be borne in mind that
ever since the close of the first World War, Foreign Ministers,
except at brief intervals, have exercised very little influence
over Japan's foreign policies. It was a case of the Foreign
Minister proposing and the military disposing. Foreign Ministers
have been selected not for their political strength at home
but for their effectiveness as negotiators with foreign govern-
ments. It is, therefore, a mistake to assume that Yoshida's
selection can be regarded as evidence that the Japanese Govern-
ment has been converted to ways of peace. It is doubted
whether Yoshida 1s qualified to assume a role of leadership
in present-day Japan and probably it will be some time before
we can tell where the real leadership in the new Japan rests.
CeD
JA:HLory:mls

THE SHIDEHARA CABINET
(Reorgan1zed January 12, 1946)
Reshuffle of Japanese Cabinet. An Allied
directive of January 4 ordering that the Japanese
effeot a purge of all leaders previously affiliated
with Japan's totalitarian party created consider-
able confusion and debate concerning the statu
of the Shidehara Cabinet. Mainly because of the
Cabinet's inability to take action toward solving
the increasingly pressing problems of daily live-
lihood there was a general demand that it resign
en masse rather than effect a partial reshuffle.
However, on Januery 1l, Premier Shidehara re-
portedly urged by Allied Headquarters to remain
in office, acoepted the resignation of those mem-
bers of his Cabinet who were directly affected
by the order, namely, the Ministers of Education,
Home, Agricuiture and Forestry, Transportation,
Chief Cabinet Secretary and one of the two
Ministers without Portfolios.
While the merits of the newly chosen members
installed on January 12 seem to justify treir
positions as administrative heads, it is sig-
nificant that the Japanese press is united in
voicing disappointment over the reshuffle and
expresses doubt that the new Cabinet can or will
take positive action toward reconstruotion.

weeks to form a coalition with the Social-Democrate, on

fi ence or ontain even passive cooperation from the Japanese,

nigh pitch of recent developments.

Character of Kodokai
By
Evidenee No.
Kazunari UGAKI
Kodokai was an organization which rejected ultra-nationali
and aggressive militarism that gained influence after the Manohuri
Incident,
It was an anti-war and pacifiet organization opposin
the extremist militarists.
I hereby certify that Kodokai stood
opposed to ordinery netionalistic organizations which supported
militarism.
1. After the Morld War I, in Japan, Communism spread rapidly, olass
struc le sharpened and social unrest was intensified as time
passed on.
The thou htful statesmen in Japan, therefore, had
greet apprehansion for the future of the country. At that time,
there were two roups of thinking to cope with this situation.
One was the expansionist group represented by General Giichi
TANAKA.
This roup maintained that, intensification of olas
struggle being the result of poverty caused by excessive
population, solution should be sou ht in the expansion of
territory anl emigation.
To this thinking, I opposed foreign
aggression.
I advocated thet the evils of capitalism whioh i
the cause of class strugle should be removed, exploitation of
labor be suppressed, parliamentary governnent with complete
reprosentetion of the will of the people be established, and
armament be reduced, with a view to solving the question by
international cooperation.
In other words, I tried to remove
social unrest by stabilizin the people's livelihood and
reforming the country.
For this purpose, I wished to
stabilize the living of the.farmers by solving the agrioultural
question, establishing the right of cultivation of land,

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
reducing farm rent and by rationalizing the farming enterpris
Standing on the conviction stated above, I foroibly reduoed
armament and suppressed the extremist elements of the a.
The main current of the army, therefore, vigorously opposed .
.Mr.Rikizo HIRAO supported my principle and always advised
me to suppress the extremist militarists who gained influence
after the Manchurian Incident and to purge the army.
3. The Gendarmerie es aware of this situation, and appeared to
have exercised pressure upon the Japan Femers Union led by
Mr.HIRANO. In order to face this situation, HIRAno thought it
the only effective way to let the soldiers fight the soldiers,
by orgar izine the pacifict soldiers into an impressive size
to crush the war-craving militarists. At that time, Mr.HIRANO
came to me and expressed his desire that he wished to actively
support the moveent that started among the pacifist retired
soldiers who were hostile to the aggressive militarists and who
entertained the same view as his. I cntirely agreed with hi
and supported him.
4. As Kodotai was orga: ized throuh the process stated above, the
army in power at that time made Co mander Shinji HATA of the
Gendarmerie constantly oppress Kodokai. As pressure was
especially severe upon Lt.÷General (retired) Morizo TODORCkI, its

this incident, Kodokai expressed itself more clearly as an organization

Dear Jim:
As Jacobs is scheduled to arrive from Jeoul at about 3 p.m.
today and expects to take off on the regular plane at 4:45 p.m. for
the tates, I am asking him to carry this letter to you. The enclo-
sure is a self-explanatory memorandum written for General MacArthur
concernin another difficulty which arose in connection with a pro-
posed subject for the meeting of the Council scheduled on February 18.
As  ou can well imagine, General MacArthur was unalterably
opposed to having any one from GHo appear before the Council to ex-
pound upon the reasons for the resi nation of the Katayama Cabinet
as well as upon the future Cabinet. In fact, he felt that the entire
subject is irregular and that discussion ty the Council of a matter
prinarily dealin. with internal political problems should not be
allowed. I argued with him to the effect that we should not attempt
to censor subjects which Members propose to place on the agenda, nor
should we attempt to dictate how these subjects are worded. I falt
that any subject which is placed on the agenda can be met squarely
and that we have nothing to fear from an open discussion on any
problem. The General, however, w s adamant regarding the represen-
tative of GH. and instructed me to tell Kislenko accordingly.
2-1448
The upshot of the matter was that the sutject has now been re-
worded to read "Discussion of the  easons for the esignation of the
Katayama Cabinet etc."
I might say that there is some danger that the joviet Member
may take the position that a change in the Government as a whole is
a matter which requires approval by all the Members of the Allied
Council for Japan, that he (the Joviet Member) does not agree, and
that the guestion of the formation of a new Cabinet must therefore
ve sent to the FEC for agreement. If maintained, this position
would, of course, effectively forestall for a considerable time the
formation of a new Cabinet. In my opinion such a position would be
untenabl, especially as no Fc directive is involved. ithout a
directive, the remise of the Soviet kember falls, and it can be
maintained,
James. enfield, suire,
eputy Director, fice  r astern ffairs,
pepartnent of itate,
ant

PERSONAL and CONFIDENTIAL
maintained, I believe, that a change of Cabinet not brought about
as a result of implementation of an FEC policy or by a directive
from SCAP is a matter not within the province of the Allied Council.
On the other hand, I shall, of course, allow discussion on
this subject by any or all Members although it appears to  that
General MacArthur's press release of 9 February 1948, and particularly

work, I do hope that you can do something to expedite Drumright's
arrival here. My need for someone to carry a greater share of the
administrative load is real and genuine.

13 February 1948
MEMORANDUM FOR: General MacArthur
SUBJECT:
Conversation with Acting oviet Member, Allied Counoil
for Japan, Ma jor General Kislenko.
General Kislenko called at my office at my request at 4 p.m.
today together with two interpreters. After preliminary greetings
I told General Kislenko that the Acting Jecretary-General had advised
me of his desire to place the following subject on the agende fer the
fifty-second meeting of the Counoil: "Information of the Represen-
tative of the GHQ, SCAF, regarding the reasons for the resignation
of the Katayama Cabinet and also regarding outlook and nature of the
new Cabinet which is being formed".
I then stated the following:
"Once again I am compelled to state that your proposed
subject for the fifty-second mmeting of the Counoil is ill-
advised and that it has become necessary for me to instruct
the Secretary-General not to place the subject, as worded,
on the Agenda. i had thought that the discussion in a
previous meeting regarding your attempt to force me as
Chairman to report to the Council had settled this matter.
However, I now find an attempt to force a representative of
General Headquarters, SCAP, to furnish information rearding
matters the solution of which is solely a matter for the
Japanese to determine for themselves as an internal political
issue in accordance with due constitutional process.
"I have already repeatedly stated that there is no
objection in a Member of the Council making any statement
in a meeting of the Council he might wish to make on any
subject, provided only that the subject-matter is properly
placed on the Agenda and appropriately worded.
"In any event, it is my considered conclusion tiat the
proposed subject is not one w.ich can or should be appropri-
ately open for discussion in the Allied Council. Your
attetion

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
attention is invited to the Supreme Commender's recent
statement in which, in clear and succinet language, he
explained his attitude conoerning the resignation of the
Katayama Cabinet. This statement has appeared in the
press and needs no further clarification. It certainly
needs no further discussion. It is my feeling that the
information re uested in the proposed subject has alreedy
fully appeared in the press and elsewhere, and that nothing
can be added to the vast amount of factual as well as
speculative writing whioh has already been published.
In view of the reasons which I have just stated,
I cannot agree to allow the proposed subject to be plaoed
on the agenda in its present form."
In reply General Kislenko stated that in his opinion the Allied
Council for Japan was formed by the Governments concerned for the
purpose of consulting with and advising the Supreme Commander regard-
ing major problems of the Occupation and that in his opinion a change
in the Japanese Government certainly was of sufficient importanoe to
warrant interest on the part of the Members of the Allied Couneil.
He stated that his sole reason for placing this subject on the agenda
of the Council is to obtain information from General Headqarters,
as well as from the other Members of the Council, coneerning the
reasons leading to the resignation of the Katayame Cabinet. Likewise,
he is interested in learning as much as possible concerning the form-
ation of a new Cabinet. He said that he is not satisfied with the
information which has appeared in the press and that be knowe no
other way to ber we well-informed on this subject than by raising
the question in the Council.
General Kislenko than asked why as Chairman I refused to allow
him to place subjects on the agenda of the Allied Couneil and,if it
is a matter of form rather than substance, could I advise him how to
overcome the faulty wording.
I told the General that I had upon several occasions already
stated that General Headquarters could not agree to being foroed by
any Member of the Council to make a statement of any kind; furthermore,
I had made it clear in previous discussions that I, as Chairman,
would not allow the Acting Soviet Member to force me to give a report
to the Council. On the other hand, I have also made it clear that
there is no objection whataoever to any Member properly placing 
subject for discussion on the agenda provided it is n appropriate
form.
General Kislenko

-3-
General Kislenko than asked whether I would agree to rewereing
this subject to read: "Discussion of the reasons for the resigmeéles
of the Katayame Cabinet and also regarding outlook and mature of the
new Cabinet which is being formed".
I replied that this subject is not a proper one for diseussiem
as to do so would interfere with an internal politieal issve and that
such discussion might naturally be construed as interferemse with
internal Japanese politics. General Kislenko replied thet be kmews
of no other way in which to place the subject on the agende and she
regardless of how the subject is worded, having onee been plesed 
the agenda, discussion is bound to follow. He said that were be to
limit the name of the subject to "Statement by the Soviet Nember
regarding the reasons....
..", the other Members might construe
that subject as shutting them out of the disoussion.
In view of the above and as I felt that the Chairman wdll be in
a stronger position by renlying directly to the Aoting Soviet Member's
statement, if any, I agreed to instruct the Secretary-General to
place the subject, as smended, on the agenda.
W. J. SEBALD

=== Page 136 === (confidence 1.00)

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Infomation Office
Press Release:
Immediate Release
9 February 1948
General MacArthur tonight issued the following statement:
The Prime Minister has just called to infonm me of the decision
that he and his cabinet will resign. He pointed out that whereas his
government has received no non-confidence vote by the Diet, it felt that
it lacked the legislative support which a government should have to be
truly representative of the people. The problems which it has faced are
not novel but are inherent in the Japanese situation. As with all gov-
ernments since hostilities ended, his has been confronted with the
serious political, economic and social dislocations which are a natural
consequence of the war and defeat.
"These conditions in more or less degree will continue to confront
future Japanese governments until through the combined energies of the
Japanese people and wise and prudent leadership, the forces of recovery
re-establish a self-sustaining economy and political and social stability.
This will of course take time, but decisive progress already has been
made. The action now being taken is in full accord with democratic pro-
cedure, as will be the manner in which the National Diet meets the issue.
The solution will be left to its decision, as the Occupation will continue
to regard the determination of such internal political issues as a respons-
ibility of the representatives of the Japanese people.
"Mr. Katayama and his cabinet have given the country a conscientious
and patriotic leadership, and I have every confidence that a new govern-
ment which emerges through the democratic processes ahead will be guided
by no less high principles."

UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER

March 15, 1948
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
SUBJECT:
Formation of Ashida Cabinet.
PARTICIPANTS: MATSUMOTO Takizo,
Parliamentary Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs;
W. Henry Lawrence, Jr.,
Second Secretary.
PLACE:
Office of United States Political Adviser,
March 15, 1948.
Mr. Matsumoto, who carried on all liaison between Dr. ASHIDA
Hitoshi, Prime Minister, (at his request) and this Headquarters con-
cerning the formation of the new Cabinet, has described the behind-the
scene activities which resulted in its selection on March 9, 1948 (this
Mission's airgram A-41, March 11, 1948). Mr. TOMABECHI Gizo, Secretary-
General of the Democratic Party, was to have headed the Cabinet Formation
Headquarters, but became ill so that Mr. TAKEDA Giichi was made Acting
Chief. According to Mr. Matsumoto, Mr. Takeda conducted the press con-
ferences, but Dr. KURUSU Takeo and Mr. KITAMURA Tokutara were Dr. Ashida's
lieutenants. Mr. Matsumoto, hovever, believes that the final selection
was made almost single-handed by Dr. Ashida.
On March 1, a list of twenty-eight candidates was presented to the
Government Section. These included all members of the Katayama Cabinet
except Mr. WADA Hiroo, Director-General of the Economic Stabilization
Board. His name was not included, according to Mr. Matsumoto, because
the Social Democrats of the House of Councillors insisted that all posts
allotted to the Social Democratic Party be held by Diet members of the
Party. Mr. Wada is an independent, although from time to time it has
been rumored he would join the Social Democratic Party. In addition
to members of the Katayama Cabinet, the following seven names were
listed who were finally included in the Ashida Cabinet:
TOMABECHI Gizo,
Secretary-General of the Democratic Party. (Prior to
this, Transportation Minister in the Katayama Cabinet).
KATO Kan ju,
Left-wing member of the Social Democratic Central
Executive Committee.
OKADA Seiichi,
Secretary-General of the People's Cooperative Party.
NAGAE Kazuo,
Right-wing member of the Central Executive Committee
of the Social Democratic Party and Parliamentary Vice
Minister of Education in the Katayama Cabinet.

TOMABECHI Gizo
It seemed clear from the entire discussion that followed with
Mr. Matsumoto that the individuals in General Headquarters with whom
he, and other members of the Japanese Government, discussed the suggest-
ed Cabinet list took special pains to avoid telling the Japanese Govern-
ment what it should do. Mr. Matsumoto indicated that while suggestions
and questions were raised, the final selection was emphasized as up to
the Japanese. The first problem arose both within Headquarters and
within the three parties concerned with the formation of the Cabinet in
connection with the suggested individuals for Attorney General. It was
felt that Mr. SUZUKI Yoshio, Attorney General in the Katayama Cabinet,
should continue in the post because he is considered an impartial and
sincere individual. Government Section feels that there were some ques-
tionable things in Mr. Hitotsumatsu's background and that while
Mr. Funada is a scholar and an authority on some aspects of law, he does
not measure up to Mr. Suzuhi. It, therefore, was decided that the post
of Attorney General should be given to the Social Democrats. This
instigated a reconsideration of allocation of posts because it had been
originally planned to give the post of Attorney General to either the
Democratic Party or the People's Cooperative Party.
The next thing that happened, according to Mr. Matsumoto, was that
Mr. Takeda declined to become Finance Minister because he felt the burden
was too much for his health. During the original allocation of posts,
the Social Democrats had waived that of the Economic Stabilization Board
because they had no one whom they felt could fill the post except
Mr. SUZuKI Masaburo, leader of the left-wing faction. Mr. Suzuki, how
ever, refused to take any Cabinet post. Mr. Kitamura had been suggested
as Director-General of the Board, but since Mr. Takeda refused the post
of Finance Minister, he was shifted to this latter post. The Democrats
had tried strenuously to get the Social Democrats to take over the
Ministry of Finance because they knew the left-wing faction of the
Social Democratic Party will undoubtedly cause a great deal of difficulty
in
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.96)
Encl. to
Tokyo's 20,
March 30, 1948.
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department Use Only)
-4-
in the formulation of the 1948-49 bulget. However, the Social Democrats
insisted that they have no men available for this post as well as that
of Chairman of the Economic Stabilization Board. Mr. Matsumoto aid
the Social Democrats did consider that Mr. Mizutani could have hold
these posts, but he was needed for the Conmerce and Industry Ministry.
Mr. Matsumoto pointed out that it was not felt wise to have
Dr. Kurusu continue as Finance Minister because of the protests filed
by the union workers in his Ministry. Dr. Kurusu had originally been
slated to be State Minister without Portfolio. He was, however, pr
vailed upon to undertake the post of Chairman of the Economic Stabilis
tion Board.
Mr. Katayama refused the post of Foreign Minister, although a great
deal of pressure was brought upon him by Dr. Ashida. Mr. Matsumoto, who
among others was sent to see Mr. Katayana in an effort to persuade him
to accept, said that Mr. Katayama felt he could make a greater contribu-
tion by consolidatin his party. During the conference between General
MacArthur and Mr. Katayama, when the latter told the General he and his
Cabinet were plannin to resign, Mr. Matsumoto declared the General had
iven Mr. Katayama the impression that the General would like to see him
continue in the Cabinet. Upon finding ir. Katayama adamant, Dr. Ashida
decided to hold the post concurrently in the hope that Mr. Katayama might
eventually be persuaded to accept the post. Mr. Katayama has become
adviser to the Minister for Foreign Affairs, a post which up to this time
has not been filled since the war..
The Government Section raised a question concernin the inclusion
of Mr. Morito in the Cabinet because of the publication of a book, "War
and Culture". An article published in the hristian Science Monitor,
issue of February 9, 1948, attacking Mr. Morito, was sent by Civil
Information and Education Section to Jovernment Section. The article
in the Honitor guoted some of the sections from the book. Mr. Matsumoto
said that members of Government Section stated they raised the question
of the inelus ion of Mr. Norito bec use of possible ill effect upon world

Mr. Morito, it was up to Dr. Ashiia. e was finally included because

House of Representatives, and Mr. ASANUMA Inejiro, Secretary-General
of the Social Democratic Party, to hold posts in a similar manner.
After making the changes in the proposed Cabinet necessitated by
the considerations highlighted above, the Cabinet as it now exists was
submitted to Government Section and General MacArthur on March 9.
Since no objection was raised, Mr. Matsumoto reported that he took the
list to Mr. Tomabechi who, in the meantime, had just had called to his
attention that the Finance and Economic Stabilization Board posts should
not be filled without the concurrence of the Economic and Scientific
Section of this Headquarters. After consulting with Dr. Ashida, it was
decided, in view of the fact that no objection had been raised by Gov-
ernment Section and General MacArthur, announcement should be made of
the Cabinet. The Economic and Scientific Section was contacted and
after several conferences,Mr. Matsumoto reported that everything was
straihtened out.
Meantime, Dr. Kurusu did not meet the members of the Economic
Stabilization Board which gave rise to considerable difficulties because
the members of the Economic Stabilization Board interpreted his not
taking over immediately from Mr. Wada as lack of interest. Actually,
Mr. Matsumoto said it was due to the fact that Dr. Kurusu did not wish
to proceed until he was certain the Economic and Scientific Section
would not object to his holding the post. Once everything was cloared,
he met with members of the Board who had decided to resign. Mr. Matsumoto
thinks that some members of the Board may be persuaded, particularly
Dr. TSURU Shigeto, Deputy Director-General, whom it is understood
Dr. Kurusu particularly hopes to retain.
The posts below Cabinet rank are still under consideration. It is
planned to have two parliamentary Vice Ministers in each Ministry
(24 in all), one from the House of Councillors and one from the House
of Representatives. There is some question still in Dr. Ashida's mind
as to whether or not there should be proportionate representation from
the two Houses, since the House of Representatives has a larger number
of members. Mr, Matsumoto said it was a suggestion of Government Sec-
tion, however, that there be one from each House. The Chief Cabinet
Secretary is to have two Deputies as contrasted with one in the Katayama
Cabinet. The two men are Mr. FUKUSHIMA Shitaro, who until now has been
Chief of the Central Liaison Office in Osaka, and Mr. ARITA Kiichiro,
who has been Director of the Maritime Bureau of the Transportation
Ministry. Mr. Fukushima was formerly a Foreign Service Officer and at
one time Consul in New York.
Er. Matsumoto pointed out that Mr. Nishio very much wanted the
post as Foreign Minister but Dr. Ashida did not consider him to have
the qualifications necessary for that particular post, which is coming
to be looked upon as the stepping stone to premiership. Dr. Ashida,
according to Nr. Matsumoto, feels the Ministry for Foreign Affairs is
growing in importance and, of course, with the peace treaty will assume
even greater significance. It is apparent that both Dr. Ashida and
Mr. Matsumoto consider Mr. Nishio as too much of a politician for this
particular post and to be rather naive as far as foreign affairs are
concerned.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
RESTRICTED
Tokyo, Jume 7, 1948:
Subject: Bill for Establishment and Organization of the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs.
4.002/6-148
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
>
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose a copy of an English translatios
of a bill currently submitted to the Diet by the Japanese Goveramemt,
together with one copy of the Japanese text, to establish the organi-
zation and functions of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. As the
Department may be aware, the law under which the various ministries
have been operating was valid for the period of one year from the
effective date (May 3, 1947) of the Constitution of Japan. The previous
law was therefore to have expired as of May 3, l948; but since now
6.1D
bills for the various ministries were not ready the time was extended,
first until May 31, and again unt1l June 30, 1948.
The draft of the proposed bill was brought to this Mission's
attention informmally by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Mr. ASAkAI
Koichiro, Chief of the Bureau of General Affairs, stated that it is
realized that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs is in a different status
from the other ministries in the Japanese Government beoause Japan's
foreign affairs are presently conducted by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers. The Ministry, however, obtained clearance from the
Government Section of this Headquarters for writing a bill under which
8
a normal Ministry of Foreign Affairs might eventually operate. Considera-
tion was given to making mention of the fact that many of the functions
M
are necessarily in abeyance until such time as the Occupation ends, but
on the advice of the Japanese Attorney-General such reference was felt
unnecessary since the authority of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers in any event takes precedence over legislation even without
specific provisions therein.
The proposed bill was submitted by the Government Section to th
for review and comment. This Mission suggested the word "directed" ror
the word "demanded" in Article 3 (13), and erpressed its concurrence in
the bill as proposed.
It is
JUL 30 1948

588

there mey be a question cemeeraing the fusetien deseribed in Artsele 8

to the Foreige Office and its foreign serviee the maistemense and pre-

Ministry of Commerce and Industry is reported to desire. to have jurisdiessem
over matters relating to foreign commerce and to heve its owm eemmereial
organisations abroad. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs, om the ether hend,
wishes to adopt the United States practice of having such matters under the
jurisdiction of a single foreign service.

(COPT)
Bill for Institution of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs
Chapter I.
General Rules
(Institution)
Article l. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Gaimusho) shall be instituted
by this law.
The head of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs shall be the
Minister for Foreign Affairs (Gaimu Daijin).
(Organs)
Article 2. The Ministry of Foreign Affaire shall consist of the
Ministry Proper and of Diplomatic and Consular Establishmente Abroad.
Chapter II.
Ministry Proper
(Functions and Authority of the Ministry Proper)
Article 3. The scope of functions within the competence of the Ministry
Proper is defined below, and the Ministry Proper shall perform the
said functions by acting within the limits of its authority and
in accordance with treaties and international laws and regulations,
as well as national laws (including orders issued under laws).
(1) Planning, formulation and execution of foreign policy.
(2) Participation in international organizations and conferences,
and promotion of international cooperation.
(3)
Conclusion of treaties and other international agreements
and disposal of matters of international law and other
international legal affairs.
(4)
Disposal of matters relating to the dispatch and reception
of Diplomatic Representatives and Consular Officers.
(5)
Maintenance and promotion of interests relating to
foreign commerce and navigation.
(6)
Protection of Japanese nationals abroad.
(7)
Disposal of matters relating to travel and emigration to
foreign countries.
(8)
Issuance and visaing of passports.
(9)
Research and documentation relating to foroign countries.
(10)
Promotion of international understanding through the dissemination
of information and knowledge relating to conditions at home and
abrond, and through the development of culturel relations with
other nations.
(11)
Liquidation of matters relating to Korea, Formosa, Saghalien,
the former leased Territory of Kwantung, the former mandated
South Sea Islands and other areas.
(12) Arrangements
RESTRICTED

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.98)
80 Jume 7, 1948.
RESTRICTED
-2-
(12) Arrangments for the repatriation of overseas Japamese
and for the expatriation of foreigmers in Japem.
(13) Investigation, custody and disposal of ertieles and property
to be restituted as dmanded by the Supreme Cemmander for
the Allied Powers, and handling of other matters relating
to Civil Property.
(14)
Collection and compilation of reccords and dooumemts relating
to the ccupation and control of Japan by the Allied Pewers.
(15)
Training of diplomatic and consular service persommel.
(16)
Disposal of mmtters relating to much imtre-Mimistry personnel,
finance, communications and miscellaneous duties as ere
connected with administrative affairs withim the competenee
of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs.
(17)
Disposal, coordination and adjustment of mmtters relating
to international relations, other than those mentioned under
the preceding numbers.
Chapter III.
Diplomatic and Consular Estal ishments
Abreed
(Functions, etc. of Diplomatic and Consular Establishments Abroed)
Article 4. The Diplomatic and Consular Establishments Abroad are under
the control of the Minister for Foreign Affairs, and shall
respectively, in foreign countries, take charge of the functioms
assigned to the Ministry Proper.
Article 5. The Diplomatic and Conmular Establishments Abroad shall, for
the time being, be governed by the provisions of laws and ordinanees
relating to much establishments, which have hitherto been in force,
except where otherwise provided for by laws or Cabinet Orders.
Appointment and dismissal of Ambassadors Extraordinary and
Plenipotentiary and Envoys Extraordinary and Ministers Plenipotentiary
shall be attested by the Emperor.
Supplementary Provisions
Article 6. This law shall come into effect as from June 1, 1948.
Article 7. The Ordinance Relating to the Organization of the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs (Imperial Ordinance No. 258, 1898) shall cease
to be velid. It is provided, however, that, except where otherwise
provided for by laws (including orders issued under laws), the
existing organs and their personnel shall be deemed to be the same
as the corresponding organs and their personnel under this law and
shall continue to function as such with the same status.

that democratization is making definite progress in Japan.
The Nippon Times editorial of October 7 elaborated this
point by stating thut it is significant that, in only the
third year after surrender, a determined effort has been
started to clean up corruption wi thout fear of the
consequences. It further emphasized that this is a tribute
to the essential soundness of the Japanese nation, henee the
Showa Denko case may prove to be a blessing in disguise.

Even Fremier May Be Examined, Say Prosecutor HORI
The arrest of ESb Chief KUkUsU is based upon good grounds and
if kURUSU's examinaticns disclose some facts involving top leaders
of the Government, the prosecution will not hesitate to examine even
the Premier, Prosecutor HUhI declared yesterday. He also asserted
that the prosecution authorities will probe into all rumors in con-
nection with the Showa Denko scandal to ascertain whether or not
the rumors are tue. Following are the questions and answers
exchanged between the press and Prosecutor HOkI:
uestion: Is the arrest of kunusU well founded?
Answer: Of course. The prosecution authorities would not
swing into action on groundless rumors. When a high Government
official like kUnUSU breaks the law, it is most likely that he has
fortified himself against exposure. Once, when high Government offi-
cials were involved in scandals, cases brought up against them by
the prosecution authorities were very often defeated in the Court
because adequate investigations could not be made. That is why we
are very cautious in the present case.
Question: If you have sufficient grounds for his arrest, why
have you requested him to report to the prosecution office volun-
tarily?
Answer: We had the warrant of arrest ready for him, but a man
of his standing would not run away, so we courteously asked him to
report to us without warrant of arrest.
Question: were the bribes accepted by KURUSU as donations to
his party?
Answer: I am not yet in a position to say anthing about that.
Question: Will there be further arrests of higher-ups in the
Government?
Answer: If, after examing KURUSU, facts which involve other
Government leaders are disclosed, we will not hesitate to examine
even

(JIJI SHIMPO)
KUnUSU heceived Several Hundred Thousand Yen from HINOHARA
In tne period of one year, from the beginning of 1947 to the
beginning of the present year, during which time KURUSU climbed up
from the position of a director of the Industrial Bank of Japan to
the position of president and finally to the post of the Finance
Minister, he is suspected of having received bribes from HINOHARA,
both in kind and money, amcunting to several hundred thousand yen.
During this period KUkUSU, together with Vice President NINOMIYA
of the Industrial Bank of Japan, who was recently arrested, held
leadership over the Reconstruction Finance Bank. The following
facts have already been investigated by the Metropolitan Police:
At the beginning of last year, when KUhUSU ran for the House
of Councillors from YA.AGUCiI, while he was one of the directors
of the Industrial Bank of Japan, he received large sums of money
including approximately 200,000 yen from HINOHAhA, who was then the
president of the Japan Hydrogen Company, as election campaign
expenses. On April 3 of the same year, HINOHARA became the presi-
dent of the Showa Electric Company on the recommendation of both
KURUSU and NINulk.IYA. At the time the Showa Electric Company had
already borrowed its first loan of 799,000,000 yen from the Recon-
struction Finance rank. Having found the loan insuffieient to
manage the company, ilivonahA was anxious to obtain a further loan.
In July of the same year, he succeeded in securing a second loan of
550,o00,o00. However, having found that funds were still short,
HIN0HAhA again obtained a third loan of 1,280,000,000 yen from the
Reconstruction Finance bank at the end of last year. During this
period, considerable sums of money, art objects, curios, rice,and
other goods were given to KUhUst. In addition to these bribes
KUhUSU is accused of having received l00,O0O yen from HINOHARA in
connection with trouble which arose between the Showa Electric
Company and the kondo Gumi in connection with the purchase of the
Akasaka building where the head offices of the Showa Electric Company
are located. HINOHAhA had made an arrangement with the Kondo Gumi,
the proprietor of which was former President MORI of the Showa
Electric Company to buy the Akasaka building for 7,Ooo,oo0 yen but
he could not pay the money. So he asked kUhUsU to mediate in the
trouble.
The Showa Electric Company is capitalized at 244,000,000 yen,
of which 152,500,000 yen is !aid up, and is the largest fertilizer
company in this country. The company has five fertilizer departments,
inciding sulphate of ammonia, nitro-lime, aluminum, and others,
and has a total of 15 plants all over the country.' The total amount
of loans obtained by the company from the Reconstruction Finance Bank
in three loans aggregated 2,638,630,000 yen for reinstatement of
production equipment. However, owing to the fact that most of the
plants were destroyed by war, the amount was diverted for the recon-
struction of their Kawasaki plant and five of their nitro-lime plants.

when HINoHan became the president of the company in

as a sort of a watchdog, plottedthe scandel. Acoordingly, of

What Does Showa Soandal Reveal?
sahi - October 1, 1948. Translator: S. Hirata
Full Translation:
What on earth has been the propellant of JaPaN's
administration for the past several months?
Is it policy? What comes first to mind, in this con-
nection, is the revision of the National Public Service
Law. This issue, however, is one which was taken up
politicelly as & result of General MecaRThUR's lettor.
It has brought to light the fact that JaPaN has not yet the
political capacity to settle this type of issue independently.
Are there any other cases in which administration
showed independent activities revolving around policies?
It may be safe to say there are none excepting the issue
of compiling the budget for the current fiscal year in
conjunction with the postponement of interest payment on
war bonds and the raising of railway fares and postal
rates. For all that, there are abundant economic and
political problems which JaPaN has to tackle squarely, such
as inflation, production increase, and introduction of
foreign capital.
If JAPaN's political scene for the past few years
has not been centered around political problems, what has
it been centered around and what has been its motive power?
It has been nothing but a series of scandals linked with
politics and shady money. This can be affirmed
positively. Take each event of the past in the political
field. We would find without fail that the dark side of
some scandal or other is casting a dark shadow over each
event. The political situation has been developing,
revolving around such issues as those regarding political
contributions, State Minister NISHIo, and state control of
coal mines, all of which have been taken up by the Illegal
Property Transactions Committee and made public one after
another. It is said that in the Diet only this committee
has been active.
Now there has cropped un the Showa Denko case. Among
the top-bracket political, financial, and government figures
who have already been arrested are the Showe Denko president,
a former agriculture and Forestry vice-minister, the Finance
Ministry's accounts Bureau chief, a vice-president of the
Industrial Bank, a managing director of the Yasuda Bank, and
a top leader of the Democratic Liberals.
Director-general KURUSU of the economic Stabilization
Board was arrested yesterday. KUrUsU's position in the
Ashida Cabinet is something more than that of a state
minister. Judging from the progress of cabinet making, or

Showa Denko Soandal and What it Teaches Us (Trans-
lator: J. Wada. JJY) - Akahata - 29 September 1948
Full Translation:
The Showa Denko Case, which came to the fore with the
exposure of the company's diversion of fertilizer into
illegal channels, devolved into a plot of HINOHan and his
followers to take over the Showa Electric Company with the
support of the Industrial Bank, and then to the
reconstruction Finance Bank's i1legal loan of 3,000,000,000
yen to the same company. This case is attracting wide
attention from the people.
Of the culprits who are involved in this blackmarketing,
plot, and corruption, ONO, MaTSUOKa, NINOMIYA, and FUKUDA
have already been arrested. The eye of suspicion is now on
the Democratic trio--SHIDa, KURUSU, and KITAMURA.
With the people's struggle becoming more vigorous, this
development of the Showa Denko case is having shocking
effects upon the ruling class and its authority. The ruling
class is now making frantic efforts to suppress the rising
popular struggle and to curb the development of the Showa
Denko case within a certain limit. The reported Attorney
General SUUKI's interference with the Prosecutors Office, his
declaration of KUrUSU's non-relation with the case, and the
company counsels' attempt to influence the Prosecutors Office
--these facts are evidence that the ruling class, which dreads
further development of the case tnd the people's indignation,
is trying to hush up the case.
will this policy which represents the character of the
ruling class prove successful? No, the people will not stand
for it. For, the corruption which has been revealed in the
Showa Denko Scandal is not & contingent limited to the Showa
Llectric Company. It is rooted deep and wide.
The amount of B loans 1s already 89,200,000,000 yen,
and the showa company is not the only one which obtained RFB
loans illegally. The itsui Mining Company, the Mitsubishi
Mining Company, the Japan lectric Generation and Trans-
mission Company, and meny other big enterprises have been
granted loans as large as those to the Showa lectric Company,
and all of these loans involves as much irregularity and
corruption as the showa loans. They do not have the
slightest intention of repuying rE loans. They are resorting
to all means of bribery and trickery to obtain shares of the
gift. Further, they are struggling to loot war termination
expenditures, publio enterprise expenditures, and price ad-
justment funds. It is evident that this struggle and
corruption will give rise to a second or third Showa Denko
case. Corruption and demoralization are the fundamental
features of the declining Japanese capitalism.

Full Translation:
The early settlement of the present political orisis
resulting from the resignation en bloo of the ashida Cabinet
seems very difficult, since the big three, the Demoorats,
the Socialists, ana the Democretic Liberals, have been hard
hit by the Showa Denko whirlwind. oreover, various
objective circumstances have to be taken into consideration
in settling the nation's political situation.
as a formal procedure, hief binet secretary
TOaBrCHI is to renort the resignation of the Premier to
Lower House Speaker MTSUOka, who will take the initiative
in mediating among the various parties.
The stands of the major parties on the present politioal
situation have not yet been rixed.
The Peoples Co-operative Party called for the establish-
ment of a super-party cabinet excluding the communists
yesterday afternoon. The party claimed that the next cabinet
should deal only with important bills, such es the revision
of the National Public Service Law. since the condition 1s
that the dissolution of the Diet will follow immediately,
the purty-proposed cabinet will be nothing more than a
oubinet supervising a general election.
Meanwhile, the attitude of the Socialist Party remains
to be clarified with central committee meetings soheduled
for Thursday and Fridey. Chairman KnTaYaa personally
entertains the view that the establishment of a super-party
cabinet, which would merely supervise the forthcoming
election, would be one way to urmount the present deadlock.
On the other han, the vemocratic Liberl Party, the
biggest opposition, has been inclined to establish a
conservative amelgumation headed by President YOsHIDa. The
purty will not easily concede to the proposal for a super-
purty cabinet. nowever, some circles predict that the
premiership of YosiI is unuesirable in view of objective
circumstances. In thi: respect, the party will show
willingness to co-operate with other conservative parties,
if other parties should proose Chief Secrettry YaaakI
for the premiership. iven in this case, the party will
oppose the formetion
ntional coalition cabinet.
Jertain members or t eloeratie larty have pinned
their hopes on Democratic Liberal Iresident YosHIsa, while
others have supporteu the retirement of old-line leaders,
such as YosHIDa and HIDl.u.. However, they unanimously
agreed that a Jemocratic Liberel cabinet should succeed
the ashide Cabinet.

as the result of the emergency session of the directors'

to reconsider its principle, if other parties should designate

after the meeting, Chairman HOSHIJIM and Vice Chairman

at all events, the Democratic Liberal Party has made up

fundamental policies.
as a measure to settle the present political crisis, some

coalition cabinet under the leadership of the Demooratio
Liberal Party, since the formation of a second Yoshida cabinef
is undesirable. Furthermore, the formation of a Katayama
Cabinet has now become impossible.

This is the more true as the  inister's misconduct occurred

Further repercussions of the affair appeared on
December 17 when, according to press reports, forty-five
women members of the Conciliation Committee of the Tokyo
Court of Domestic Relations demanded the resignation of
Mrs. Yamashita. The demand was made in a joint letter to
hadio Tokyo which was read over the air on the Voice of the
People program. The signatories deplored the alleged damage
to the honor and dignity of Japanese womanhood done by Mrs.
Yamashita through her involvement in the incident. Another
repercussion was the institution of a suit by a Democratic-
Liberal member of the Lower House against a Democratic
member for threatening him with violence during a quarrel
concerning Mrs. Yamashita on the night of the incident.
Izumiyama, whose career embraced managerial and research
positions with the itsui and Teikoku banks, did not enter
politics until April 20, 1948, when he was elected to the
House of hepresentatives from Yamaguchi Prefecture. He was
appointed to the posts from which he has just resigned on
October 19, 1948. To fill temporarily the vacanc1es
occasioned by the resignation of Izumiyama from his Cabinet
positions, the present Commerce and Industry Iinister and
the Agriculture and Forestry Minister, respe ctively, have
been appointed to the Finance and Economic Stabilization
Board posts. HInunn.A kozen, Chief Secretary of the Democratic-
Liberal farty, stated to an officer of tnis Mission that a
successor to fzumiyama will be selected as quickly as is
possible under the circumstances and that a reliable person
of influence anu importance outside the Government is most
likely to be designated.
The exaggerated press response to the ineident has
been reported in tnis Mission's despatcn no. 788, December 17,
1948. It is of Interest that the Communist Farty organ,
the Akahata, uid not mention the affair editorially.

The Honcrable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
have the honor to report that the Japanese Cabinet, in
consideration of current threats to he maintenance of order and
J
the possible necessity of nat ional action with regard theroto,
has established a C. pinet Committee on Public Safety comprised
of the members of the Cabinet particularly concerned wn the
public peace and certain other members of approximate Cabinet
rank. The Committee has been set up on the authority of the
Cabinet without specific legislation. Subordinate tothis Cabinet
Comnittee there has also been established a Freparatory Committee
at the ministerial working level, comprised of the chiefs of the
respectiv General Affairs Bureaus in the ministries concerned.
The Preparatory Committee is particularly active and has been
meeting t o or hree times weekly. Many proposed decisions which
are generally agreed to between the ropresentatives of the ministries
are finally determined upon without reforring to the Cabinet Committee.
The Prime Minister has indicated an interest in keeping this
Mission informed of pertinent inforinntion which comes before the
Preparatory Committee and the Cabinet Comnittee and with this ob
jective in mind has instructed the Chief of the General Affairs
Bureau of the Foreign Office to keep in touch with Misslon officers
in this connection. The two committees and their oporations are
confidential and no reports of them have reached the press.

Information conceming the Personnel Reduction of Government Railwax
1. The situation surrounding the aecond reduotion.
The workers will be notified of their discharge between the
15th and the 20th of this month at the various headquartors through-
out Japan. It is still unknown whether the discharged workers will
resort to strike in their protest against the personnel reduotion,
but if the strike occurs it will be in the most probability around
the fifteenth or the sixteenth of this month. Aocording to one source
of information, it is said that the Communists have verbally ordered
to carry out the strike,in the various sectors on the 14th, 15th, and
the l6th of this month. Other sources claim that orders have been al-
ready issued to strike on the 15th midnight. However, the leaders of
the'Communist Party are at present travelling in remote parts of Japan
and it is reported that they are scheduled to return around the 17th.
Viewed from this fact, the strike may occur between the 17th and the
 20th of this month.
2. United otruggle with other uniona.
The Government Railway Workers Union's cajol i for united
struggle have not yet materialised. The Tokyo-Shibaure (To-Shiba)
Electric Union have not sufficiently prepared to the present date
and the Zentei (All Communication Workers Union) in the all proba-
bility will not join the common struggle. The details are unknown,
but it is said that the League of Koreans led by Kim have already
made an overture for the unitod action.
3. Tokuda'a astivitiea in Oaaka araa.
The most vociferous leader of the Communist triumvirate, Kyuichi
Tokude, arrived in Osaka and is reported to have said the follordng:
1. Object of the struggle
The workers should oxert their all-out offorts to fight after
the llth when the notice of demotion and second personnel reduotion
takes place.
2. Method to be amployed
Rallies, negotiations by collective bargaining, desertion from
work, and other means.
3. Strategy
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch No.
475, July 22, 1949.
-2-
3. Strategy
Collection bargaining must be conducted with the responsibl
person in each unit. If the police are numerically weak, physical
resistance should be employed. However, if outnumbered the strikers
should flee from the police.
4. Efforts must be concentrated at Suita.
5. A person will be dispatched from the Communist Headquarters on
the 15th to lead the agitation.
6. Strike order should not be delivered if possible.
The Government Railways have been giving priority to repatriates
who had formerly worked in the Railways, but if such a priority 1s.
given it will become extremely disturbing as the Soviet-eduoated men
will doubtlessly create further troubles. Therefore, the Railways
authorities are studying the new measures to cope with the dituation.

National Railway Trouble and Attitude of Koreans in Japan
There is an unmistakable tendency among the Koreans in Japan
toward identifying themselves with the great labor offensive, of
which the National Railway Workers Union is the prime mover. Those
belonging to the Association of Korean Residents are assuming a
non-intervention or a hands-off attitude, but those of the League
of Koreans in Japan are endeavoring to aid and abet the sumor-to-
autumn labor offensive by throwing the whole of their wall-organised
strength into the struggle. Furthermore, they are apparently trying
to take this opportunity to realize their own sinister desigms.
Their strategy and tactics in the concrete are considered herounder.
The 17Oth Session of the Central Executive Committee of the
League of Koreans in Japan was held for three days, beginning wdth
June 25th last. Among the principal iteme on the agenda were the
following:
1. How to vindicate the right of living of Koreans in Japan,
and how to promote the "democratio" revolution of Japan, by effeotive
ly utilizing the present labor offensive?
2. What methods of propaganda and a campaign of disputes are to
be adopted by the League of Koreans in Japan in collaboration with
the "Fatherland Unification 'Democratic' Front" (whioh was in conference
in North Korea at the time), with respect to the development and prog-
ress of the unification movement following the withdrawal of United
States troops from South Korea?
As a result of deliberation on these matters, tho Central Executive
Committee of the League of Koreans in Japan decided upon conorete steps
to be taken in conducting disputes in the forthooming labor offensive,
and they ore ciroularised throughout the country in a direotive dated
June 27th. They are:
1. Stick to death to the workplaoe.
2. Carry on disputes for acquiring educational expenses.
3. Carry on disputes for rectification of unreasonable taxation.
4. Carry on diaputes in order to get the sale of staple food on
credit recognized.
5. Positively assist the disputes conducted by the National Rail-
way Workera Union.
Consoquenoes
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 3 to
Tokyo's Despatch No.
475, July 22, 1949.
-2-
Consequences of this provocative attitude of the League of
Koreans in Japen have already been seen in the recent inoidents
at the Tokyo Metropolitan Office, in Hiroshima and in Taire.
In pursuance of a decision taken by the Central Executive
Committee of the League of Koreans in Japan, a "Great Blocs Gon-
ference", with the whole country divided into elght bloos, is to
be held in mid-July. On the agenda are the following:
1. Attitude toward the fatherland funification ic movement.
2. Methods of carrying on disputes in the present labor of-
Fenaive in Japan.
3. How to celebrate effectively August l5th (liberation com-
memoration day)?
4. Disputes for acquiring educational expenses and for safe
guarding livelihood.
It is to be noted that, as will have been seen from the above,
the League of Koreans in Japan, with an estimated membership of
600,000, contemplates bringing the full weight of its extemmively-
organized power to bear upon the labor situation in Japan.
More recently, it appears that, with the intensifiostlon of
disputes resulting in some Koreans themselves suffering the evil
consequences of their own doings, the League of Koreans in Japan
has come to re-examine its tactics. It is logioal to assume that
hereafter the League will assume tactios less overt and less vocal
than formerly, yet even more subtle and sinister, and caloulated
to achieve its essential objects in view.

COPY
1. Information on the proposed personnel adjustment of the National
Railway Corporation.
Generally speaking, the situation is quiet. It appears the
swift steps taken by the authorities have somewhat forestalled any
moves. Disagreement within the labor union apparently 1s. causing
much anxiety to the Central Struggle Committee. As reported in the
newspaper, the members of the Democratization League withdrew from
the yesterday's meeting of the Central Struggle Committee. Sub-
sequently the members of the League proposed to the Central Struggle
Committee: (1) to withdraw the so-called Atami resolutions; and
(2) to convene a meeting of the Central Committee, and gave notice
that if their proposal was not agreed to they would take decisive
steps to organize a new labor union. Depending upon what attitude
the Central Struggle Committee will take in regard to the above-
mentioned offer, the union might split into two.
The local situation was like this: In the Tamachi Eleotrio
Car Pool some extermal forces were working, but no serious moves
were perceived. The situation in Kansai district and westward is
particularly quiet. For a time some degree of unrest was caused
in  orioka area but was quieted down. Hokkaido is generally calm.
"In Kinazawa the maneuvering from out side has succeeded in giving
rise to some measure of disquietude. In Akita an article which
appeared in the Chokai Geppo entitled: "Those dismissed are unruly
elements," gave rise to some disturbances. Particular care should
be exercised in handling press reports in the future.
As regards the Mitaka railway accident, deaths amounted to
nine, while the cases of injuries are still to be ascertained. The
latter are expected to amount to a considerable number. Obvioualy
the accident was not one of spontaneous nature. Arrangements are
being made for arresting the suspects.
2. Moves of the National Communications Workers Union.
In Hiroshima area, cables were severed. Similar incident
occurred between Okayama and Himeji. Yesterday an underground cable
was severed in Tokuyama. In so far as the National Communications
Workers Union is concerned, the district is the noisiest. The time
for discharging the communications workers has not as yet been de
cided. It is considered the proper time would be after the streamlining
of the National Railway workers is completed.

an editorial in the Nippon Times of October 16, 1948,
3
five copies of which are enclosed, aptly describes the
need of confidence in the new administration and points
out some auspicious factors connected with the designation
of Mr. Yoshida as Prime Minister, including the speed of
designation, the wide appeal of much of his program, and
his forthright attitude. Despite these favorable aspeets
the editorial expresses the opinion that his regime is
likely to be a short-lived and stormy one beeause
Mr. Yoshida does not command a majority in the House of
CONFIDENTIAL

strains exist.
Reasons for Popularity of Demooratic
Liberal Party
The comments of . tsumoto as to the reason for
the popularity of the Democratic Liberal Party are worth
noting. One retson ascribed by him derives from the dis-
appointment of people wi th the lack of achievement of the
perties recently in power, another results from the
promises of the Democratic Liberal Party to bring a speedy
improvement in conditions by the removal of economic
controls. pecifio promises included abolition of the
transactions tax, free sale of farm produce, once the
delivery yuotas tre offered in full, the re-opening of
restaurants, and the easing of money stringenoy.
 third reason suggested is the support given by
some newspapers to r. Yoshida's candidacy for Prime
inister. ccording to r. tsumoto, these newspapers
regard Mr. Yoshida as the one Japanese leader who will
go to General Macarthur and speek frankly. We believe
these newspapers are convinced that Mr. Yoshida is not
completely subservient to the Occupation and for this
reason regard him as & kind of symbol of the increasing
desire on the part of the Japanese to become more
independent. In our opinion, this attitude on the part
of newspapers is additional evidence of the noticeable
and increasing restlessness of the populace under the
Occupation, already reported in this Mission's despatch
no. 589 of September 9, 1948 transmitting a resolution
requesting the early conclusion of a peace treaty. support
for r. Yoshida by other newspapers, according to Mr.
Matsumoto, was largely on parliamentary grounds, their
contention being that . Yoshida, es head of the
opposition (and not necessarily from eny personal popularity),
should have taken over immedi&tely upon resignation of the
ashida Cabinet. This is the point of view taken in the
asahi editorial of October 15 mentioned above.
ttitude Concerning Timing of
Diet Dissolution
The present opposition parties hope that, before there
is & general election, the Yoshida cabinet wiil remain in
power long enough to show that its promises are illusory.
These parties believe that as opposition parties they hold
the key to the timing of the dissolution. In onneetion
with this timing, an editorial in the Nippon Times of
4/
October 21, 1948, five copies of which are enolosed, 1s
pertinent. It points out that, while the man in the street
hopes for an early election, professional politicians of
the opposition parties would like to see it postponed as
long as possible. The latter consider not only that they
must have time to re-group their forees, but also (and

probably

time until some new scandal will besmireh the reputmss

already been besmirehed.
The editorial also makes the same point as Mr.
Matsumoto that the longer the Demoeratic Liberal Party is
in power the more unpopular it will become. This is bamed
on the belier that the times are so difficult that no party
can satisfy the people. The editorial prognosticates that
there is a reasonable possibility that the present cabinet
mey last into the spring of next year, an opinion shared
by. tsumo to.
It should be noted that rticle 69 of the Constitution
refers to possible dissolution of the riouse of Representa-
tives ufter passage of a non-confidence resolution or
rejection of & confidence resolution. No specifio
provision is made for jissolution simply in the Cabinet's
judgment. It would therefore &ppear that the House of
representttives is in position to defer the possibility
of dissolution if it is willing to avoid passing a non-
confidence resolution or rejecting & confidence resolution.

Yoshida hxpression of Desire

Mr. Yoshida appears to be aware that he is not po
with the Covernment section of this Headquarters (this
Mission's despttch no. 431 of July 10, 1948). He has.
however, stated that he wishes to cooperate with the

subjeot of cooperation, on October 12, 1948 (prior to
the election of Mr. Yoshida) between Mr. Masuda and an
officer of this mission, Mr. Lasuda gave emphasis to the
assertion that his party desires to cooperate with this
Headquarters (particularly the conomic and Sointirio
Section) and to look to the United states for continued
εid and guidance. The cooperation with the Eoonomio and
Soientiric Section was apparently mentioned to indicate
that while his ptrty stood for a middle-of-the-road poliey,
it hoped to remove controls as rapidly as economic recovery
permitted, ut the same time recognizing fully that a laissez
faire course could not be pursued.
UY
r. Yoshida was elected Prime Minister
despite efforts of the Democratic Party,
People's cooperative Party, and a group with-
in the Democratic Liberal Party to elect Mr.
Yamazaki, seoretary General of the latter
party. This move was nullified when Mr.
Yamazaki resigned from the House of nepresen-
tatives (from among the members of which the
Prime inister must be designated). kfforts
to form a national coalition cabinet also
failed when r. Yoshida refused to commit him-
self to trying to form such a oabinet.
Despite the downfall of the shida Cabi-
net, the Democratic Party is not expected to
disintegrate, but might merge with the
Democratic Liberal Party if the latter party
were re-organized.
The Yoshida Cabinet is expected to be
short-lived and to have & stormy tenure. Its
popularity may also decline, because its
promises of removal of economio controls are
praotically impossible of achievement under
existing oonditions.
The magnitude of the problems oonfronting
the Yoshida Cabinet are epitomized in the
confliot between the people's expeotation of
drastio economio decontrol and the necessity
for strict enforcement of certain ontrols to
wipe out corruption. In addition, there is

Foreign Office has on confidential basis provided me
with draft of Prime Minister's coming policy state-
ment (to be delivered December 13) relating to foreign
affairs. After remarking upon progress made in par-
ticipation international conferences and increase in
numbers Japs going abroad he proposes saying following
regarding peace:
"Thanks to favorable actions of SCAP authorities during
past 4§ years and assiduous labors day-in and day-out
740.0011
peace. What is desired is restoration free intercourse
P.210.0
ith other countries, which is not only economically
advantageous but also psychologically beneficial in
that it will give our narion hope and confidence and
kindle its enthusiaam for working out its new destiny.
In this sense, I cannot overstress importance of
spirituai effect on peace treaty upon our people. I
hope peace will be concluded at earliest possible date;
and I earnestly hope it will be concluded with as many
powers as possible.
"Concerning peace treaty so devoutly wished for by our
nation I have already stated my views during last ses-
